- A local write's output that fails its output schema comes back instead of being dropped: as the result of a confirmed write or one that sent nothing, and as the unconfirmed output of an `outcome_unknown` write. One whose declared commit steps were never entered fails as `invalid_output`, `not_applied`, unless its caller left a popup's question unanswered during the run. A read's still fails as `invalid_output`.
- The local MCP server marks a read tool idempotent, and `provide_input`, `cancel_job` and `mint` as writes. A run call whose job failed returns `isError` and keeps its `structuredContent`. A tool's input no longer repeats a summary of its fields when they are listed beside it. `pomerado/core/mcp/schema` exports `readHints`, `writeHints` and `nestedInput`.
- A local run asks its caller only about a write's confirm that its build didn't record, as one yes or no question worded for the dialog, and gives every other popup the safe default. It used to ask Accept or Dismiss about each one, and a prompt's text as a secret. A cancel at the terminal still stops the step, and builds keep their own question. `askDialogCaller` in `pomerado/core/browser/dialogs/expected` asks it for any host.
- `scriptAnswers` in `pomerado/core/destinations/card-fill` hands a script its answers with each card answer replaced by a handle, as the local host does.
- Guardian's source reads and step context are shared by every host.
  - `pomerado/core/guardian/source` exports the SDK path mapping: `operationReviewSources`, `hostProvidedSource`, `sdkJavaScriptPath`, the `SdkFiles` type, `sdkReference`, which answers a guessed SDK path with where the SDK is, and `hostReference`, which wraps such an answer as one chunk.
  - `pomerado/core/guardian/review` exports `refusalsOnHostGapsFail`. A deny or escalate after a failed read of a file the host provides fails as that read's `SourceUnavailable`, an outage, instead of a refusal.
  - `pomerado/core/mint/review-context` exports `explorationAllowedEffect` and `cappedStepResult`.
  - The local host uses all of them. A guessed path under an SDK folder (`runtime/`, `browser/`, `testing/`, `node_modules/pomerado/` or `pomerado/`) now reads as a `host_sdk_reference` pointing at `pomerado/browser/index.js`. A TypeScript spelling maps to its JavaScript only under those folders. A read that finds no file names its `pathCategory`. The local secret check's refusal of a file is not a host gap, since it refuses the file on every read, so a Guardian refusal after one stands.
  - The exploration allowed-effect text adds that clicking the site's own sign-in link or button is navigation, and so is a context step after sign-in that asks for no credential, such as choosing a supplier, company, client or language. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact request text needs recording again.
- The local host takes three build-side pieces that every host now shares, so a local build behaves like a hosted one:
  - A failed act step Guardian labelled a write asks the minter to read back before any further write unless its commit marks show it entered no commit step, with the shared notice text: the new `readBackFirst` in `pomerado/core/mint/write-session`. The local host records no requests that would show a write the page sent before any mark was entered, so it skips the ask on those marks only when the step also made no browser call. The local host used to ask whenever the step made a browser call, entered a mark or lost its result. It now also asks after a labelled write that declares no commit step and failed before any browser call, telling the minter the write may already be committed. `commitUncertain`, `uncertainCommit` and `verifyFirstNotice` stay exported, deprecated.
  - `exampleOutputEvidence` in `pomerado/core/mint/publication-review` records the output as JSON rather than a JSON string, and the record is compact JSON. It cuts an output past 96 KiB by whole records of its largest top-level list with `truncation` (else a prefix as before), and returns the parsed output as `screened`. A mid-size or large list output keeps more records within 96 KiB than before, and the digest covers every record. The new `outputDigest`, `outputDigestPath` and `outputDigestNote` give the review a per-field digest of each list in the output, `exampleOutputRecord` writes the record for a state such as `unavailable`, which `ExampleOutputState` adds, and `publicationScope` takes optional `hostWritten` and `references`. `PublicationScope` adds the optional `references`. The local host writes the digest and lists its skills as references.
  - `mintSourceSyntaxFailure` is back in `pomerado/core/mint/operation-source`, and the local host refuses a step whose entrypoint does not parse before review.
- The read-back notice from `readBackFirst` in `pomerado/core/mint/write-session` lets a failed write step whose commit request returned 2xx or 3xx read back the write's own result, the record it just created or changed, on another page such as a list as well as on the page as it stands. It adds that reading is allowed and a second write is not. Every host gives the same text.
- One list of credential names and key shapes serves every host. `credentialPolicy.multiWordNames` adds `secret string` and `secret binary`, and `awsAccessKeyIdPattern` also finds a key ID beside an underscore, so error messages mask both. `providerKeyPattern` finds each of two keys written one after another (`whsec_…/ghp_…`), where a body that may hold `-`, `+`, `/` or `=` used to run into the next key and leave part of it readable. The local publication precheck refuses a saved file that holds a provider key or an AWS access key ID as `source_screening`, with a `provider_credential` publication block naming the file and position.
- New `pomerado/core/privacy/value-forms` lists the forms a registered secret value can be written in: `observedForms` gives it as given, percent-, form- and base64-encoded and JSON-escaped, and `shownForms` every form a page or URL shows it in. `formDecoded` decodes form and percent escapes. The local host's run secrets now also mask and refuse a value base64-encoded, and a sign-in's sent check decodes each valid escape run in a request even beside an invalid one.
- Guardian's execution policy lets a host autofill sign-in step put slot `code` on each one-character box of a code split across boxes, when the step lists every box in order, as well as on a one-time-code field. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact policy text needs recording again.
- The auth skill says a code split into one-character boxes is one `code` field per box, in order, as a split date is one field per part, and that the host asks once and types one character into each.
- The core skill says typing or choosing can reload part of the page once per key or choice, such as suggestions or a dependent list, with answers that land out of order and an element the page may replace. It prefers waiting until every answer has landed, looking the element up again rather than reading one found earlier, checking it shows the answer to the whole value, and entering the value once more when it doesn't.
- A host that can't move a build to another site can say so up front. `MintDependencies` adds the optional `siteMoveUnsupported`. When a host sets it, the harness refuses a `site` change as `site_move_unsupported` when the minter proposes it, before the host asks for the caller's confirmation or Guardian reviews it, and tells the minter to recommend a new build for the other site. Such a host used to refuse the move only after the caller confirmed it and Guardian allowed it, and Guardian could then reject the new-build recommendation as the same task. `PendingTaskUpdate` adds the host fact `siteMoveUnsupported`, which every update review then reads, and Guardian's update policy says that when the host reports it can't move this build to another site, a change of site is a reason for a new build. A host that leaves it out keeps today's behavior. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact policy text needs recording again.
- A maintenance build that is the tool owner's own request to change the tool can loosen an output field the request names. `MintDependencies.taskUpdateConfirmer` and `PendingTaskUpdate.maintenance.confirmer` add `owner_request` (type `TaskUpdateConfirmer`): the person answering the build's questions owns the tool, and the build is their request. With it, a `mint_update` output change may cite no answer and goes to Guardian, whose task update review treats a change the request plainly names as confirmed and needs no evidence that the site stopped showing the value; it returns `clarify` when the request is ambiguous. Guardian's question review allows a question asking the owner to confirm an output change their request leaves open, and still rewords one the request already settles. A maintenance update's review now reports the host's confirmer for every update, so an update that only adds or tightens fields may show `owner` or `owner_request` where it showed `none`. Recorded Guardian responses keyed on the exact task update or question policy text need recording again. A host that switches over `confirmer` handles `owner_request` as an owner who requested the change.
- A host can check a sign-in recipe and its entry URL as one version before it publishes it, with `checkSignInVersion` from `pomerado/core/mint/sign-in-version`.
  - It decodes the version's `recipe` as a host reads it (`SignInRecipe`) and refuses one that does not decode, such as a recipe with no steps or a workspace step whose page is more than a path, as `invalid_recipe` before any other check. It then runs the deterministic checks over a `SignInVersion`, its `entryUrl` and `recipe`: the entry URL, every step's page, fields, submit, methods and rejection markers, the signed-in marker, and a version 4 or 5 recipe's workspace relation and workspace steps. It refuses a selector, page or marker that names the recording account's identity values or copies a masked contact or number, an entry URL, page or popup whose host labels below the site's registrable domain name an identity value, a step or a marker's account page (`openPath`) that signs out, an entry URL that is one authorization request or carries credentials, a marker that a signed-out page shows or that is a sign-in page's path, a workspace relation whose `parent` is not the site's registrable domain (`workspace_parent`), an entry URL, page or popup that is not https (`insecure_origin`), and an entry URL, page or popup that is not on the site's registrable domain, or a workspace step without a valid workspace relation (`foreign_origin`). Each finding names the rule and the part's path, such as `recipe.steps[2].submit`, never its value.
  - `SignInVersionInput` takes the optional `entryUrlAsked`: the host already asked about a one-time entry URL and the build kept it, so `one_time_url` does not refuse it, and only the URL's path is read for a masked number, since its query and fragment hold spent random values. The identity rules apply to the `identities` a caller passes, and to the `[private]` placeholder whatever it passes. Off-site sign-in pages are always reported under their own rule, `foreign_origin`, so a host that permits sign-in on another site's origin drops that rule's findings itself and still gets every other rule, `insecure_origin` included.
  - The sign-in recorder and the version check share one masked-contact test.
- A sign-in may move to a per-account workspace host on the site's registrable domain.
  - Sign-in recipe version 4 adds the optional `workspace: { parent }`, the site's registrable domain. `autofillRecipe` and `signInRecipe` take it as an optional last argument and write version 4 only when a recipe uses it or a workspace step, so every other recipe keeps its bytes. A version 4 recipe with a key this host does not know is refused as `invalid`. `signInRecipeText` writes `version`, `workspace`, `steps` and `signedIn` in that order, and a workspace step's `origin` before its `page`.
  - `pomerado/core/runtime/workspace-origin` exports the deterministic gate and run-time rules: `workspaceHost`, `workspaceRelation`, `pinnedWorkspaceOrigin`, `recordedScreenOrigin`, `recordedScreenPage`, `admitScreenOrigin`, `signedInCheckOrigin`, `workspaceToPin` and `entryNamesAddress`. A workspace host is an exact https origin on the site's own port, so a site on the default port admits no explicit port. `workspaceToPin` takes the same `SignedInCheckContext` as `signedInCheckOrigin` and returns a pin only for the pinned host, the host this replay learned after its identifier, or a host the site moved to after the login's password or code went out on a screen shown on its own recorded origin; a workspace host a session merely shows may be the check's origin, never the pin. `entryNamesAddress` says whether a login URL's path, query or fragment, read through up to four layers of percent-encoding, with compatibility forms such as fullwidth letters read as plain ones (NFKC) and any other full stop (such as U+3002) read as ".", names another address: an absolute or protocol-relative URL, a backslash, a host on the site's registrable domain, or text that does not decode. The URL's own host is never read. There is no rule on a workspace host's label shape, and an identifier-first sign-in may learn its workspace once the identifier went out on the shared host, at most one host per replay.
  - `signInForRun` and `replaySignInRecipe` take the login's pinned `workspaceOrigin` and an `onWorkspaceEvent` listener for private events, and a signed-in result names the `workspaceOrigin` to pin, only one the login's own sign-in explains. A replay refuses a workspace host that shows before the login sent its identifier as `workspace_unverified`, another one than the pinned host included, and a second workspace host after the one it learned, and `signInForRun` then calls the browser's optional `clearSiteData` and starts again once. Only what a screen sends on its own recorded origin counts as sent there, never the same screen admitted on the pinned host. For a recipe with a workspace relation, `signInForRun` refuses a login URL that `entryNamesAddress` flags as `RecipeFailed` with `entry_names_address` before it opens anything. `SignInBrowser.confirm` takes an optional `openOrigin` where the recipe's `openPath` opens.
  - `pomerado/core/guardian/sign-in-origin-reviews` exports the private host review kind `workspace_origin` for any host that records such a sign-in, with its evidence type and `workspaceOriginDecision`. A `workspace_origin` allow carries only the label `workspace` or `shared_host`, and a deny only `unrecognized`.
  - A Guardian `HostReview` may tie its labels to its outcomes with the optional `outcomeLabels`. A decision whose label its outcome does not carry is invalid; a kind that is not private is told once in the same review which labels its outcome carries, as for an outcome it does not return.
  - The local host replays version 4 recipes and runs the tool on the workspace its sign-in reached, for that call only. Its sign-in origins are still only the configured ones. Its `clearSiteData` clears only the site's own cookies and storage, each same-site origin's tab session storage included, and leaves other sites' data and other tabs alone. It doesn't yet record workspace relations when it builds a tool.
  - The auth, core and http-mcp skills say to record the shared sign-in host, never a workspace host or slug, with a `loginUrl` that carries no return address, and that `siteOrigin` is the account's workspace origin at run time.

- An `execute` result over 16 KiB reaches the minter as a digest, and the whole result as a file it reads with `read_source` or searches with `rg`. A result within 16 KiB comes back whole and unchanged. A larger one is saved, indented, to the read-only `captures/results/<executionId>.json` before the digest returns: its size, its shape with every array's length, its first items, its error fields (up to 20, whole within 12 KiB) and the file's path. A digest never exceeds 24 KiB or the result's own size; a result whose digest would be larger comes back whole. A failed result that names no error field keeps its first 12 KiB of text. A command's output is never digested, and Guardian's view of step results, already cut at 4 KiB, is unchanged.
  - `MintDependencies` adds the optional `saveResult(path, text)`, which saves the screened result as a read-only workspace file. A host without it, a save that fails and a digest that cannot be built return a result within 16 KiB whole and a larger one as its first 16 KiB with its size and a note that the rest was not kept, so no result reaches the minter above 64 KiB. The local host saves it as it saves its other captures.
  - The workspace guide tells the minter how to read a digest and its file.
- The writes skill tells a write that changes existing state to read it before and after the commit through the same query or view, selecting the same fields, since two read paths can return the same unchanged field in different shapes. It fails only when a field the input sets did not change as asked, and `verified()` depends only on those fields. For every other field the comparison flags only a change the session's own confirmed write did not make too: a field the site changed there on its own, such as an updated-at time, a version, a last editor or derived text, is left out, while a field the flow cleared by mistake is fixed, never left out. The rest is compared by value after bringing both reads to one shape, never as serialized text, and a reference by its ID. Once the commit is confirmed and every requested field reads back as asked, the script calls `verified()` and reports any other difference in its output instead of throwing, so a landed write never ends as a failed run; a script that confirms from the commit response takes the comparison's after read through the before read's query before `verified()`. The skill says its no-rerun rule is about running the write, not about writing checks: before `finish_build` the minter runs the composed script's read-back match and comparison with Node in `exec_command` over what the session read, its before and after reads or their saved response bodies. The check sends nothing to the site and is not a second write. It uses the act steps' own before and after reads first and captured bodies only for what they lack; a body not saved whole (pending, incomplete, omitted, withheld, unavailable, empty where the site sends one, truncated or masked) leaves that part not checked, disclosed in `coverage`, never passed; an exchange missing from the captures proves nothing until the capture's gaps, notices and every session were read; the after body is picked by the commit's time and the record's identity, skipping a service worker's duplicate; and values are compared, never serialized text. The core and testing skills and the workspace guide say so too. A write with branches, such as create or update, may make a commit call per branch but keeps their confirmation and comparison in shared code, checks a branch the session did not run offline against captured shapes, asks the owner before the first commit which branch to run live when no captured shape can stand in, and names the branch that ran in `coverage`.
- Guardian's publication review of a write now also returns a confirmation finding for a check that would reject a correct write: a before-and-after comparison of whole objects or serialized text, a read after the commit through another query than before it, a field the site maintains counted as a change, a branch the session did not run that confirms through its own code (its own commit call is no finding), or a throw after a confirmed commit whose requested fields read back as asked. A coverage claim that the checks passed offline over a body that was not saved whole is an unsupported claim. It judges these from the source and never asks for another run of the write for them. The publication skill lists the same. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact policy text needs recording again.
- A new `records` skill guides tools that read or write records or table rows in an app whose fields are user-defined or found at run time, such as a CRM's objects, database or spreadsheet rows or an app's custom objects. For those tools it takes precedence over the core and search skills' lean toward more information and one typed field per fact; a record type with fixed, known fields keeps typed fields. Such a tool returns a small default: each record's `id` and a `fields` object keyed by the field or column name people see, with only filled-in fields, links as `{ id, label }`, choices as labels and system fields left out. It offers an optional `fields` projection, and `include: ["schema"]` for compact field definitions (name, label, type, writable, choices, and a link's target and write key) or `include: ["system_fields"]`, and keys its output the way the same site's write tools take it. A projected name that matches several of the site's names ignoring case and spacing throws `InvalidInput` listing them, and the schema comes on the first page only. The build still records every field it finds. The core skill's output rules and the workspace guide point to it, and the skill catalog lists it after `pagination`.
- The workspace guide's new "Solve before you narrow" rule tells the minter to solve a capability that proves hard, such as a reference field a write first picked by its label, when the site offers a way, to ask the owner one bundled question when unsure, and to leave a capability out only when it is hard and optional to the request, saying why in the tool's description. The rule for a publication finding that looks impossible to fix, and the core skill's contract-gap rule, follow it.
- The workspace guide's "Try hard, then ask" adds an eighth case: before leaving out or refusing a capability the site offers, such as a field or column type, an option or what a review finding asks for, the minter asks the owner one batched question listing each capability, what supporting it would take and its recommendation. A review that offers refusal as an alternative is not the owner's choice, and refusing a capability the site offers is a narrowing, never a fix. Such a question is a design question, not a request for permission. Because an unanswered question ends a signed-in build, the guide says to ask design questions early, before signing in where possible, with a recommended choice. A publication finding first seen after sign-in is still asked, as one batched question with a recommended choice.
- The writes skill says a write proves the lookup its composed script needs during the act session, in an act step that changes nothing or from the site's own lookup response in the captures, since nothing runs live after it.
- The writes skill's new "Check the read-back" section has a write that sets a person, linked record or other reference look its ID up from the caller's label through the site's own list before the commit and compare IDs on the read-back. When the site shows no ID and no list to resolve one, it compares the displayed label, and when neither can tell, it asks the owner or follows the existing rule for an unverifiable write. Its before-and-after check compares values, not object identity or key order, and does not count a field the site itself maintains on every save, such as an updated-at time, as an unrequested change.
- The auth skill says every selector a `signInStep` sends, its fields, submit, methods, rejection markers and signed-in check, is a Playwright selector string, not a locator call: `role=button[name="Sign in"]` or `[name="Sign in" s]` matches the whole name, case included, as `getByRole` with `exact: true` does, `i` ignores case, and `exact` is a locator option, never a selector attribute: it fails as an unknown attribute or, after a quoted name, fails to parse. `getByLabel` and `getByText` have no string form the host runs. The `auth-entry.ts` example notes the string form beside its locator calls, and the `check_signed_in_marker` tool description says the same for the marker.
- The writes skill prefers throwing over returning when a write stops before its last commit step: a report of the form's fields, its validation messages or the options to pick from is a refusal (`InvalidInput` with `available`) or a failure (`OperationFailure`), not the write's output. A write that returned such a report reported a possible change instead of the refusal or failure. Guardian's publication policy makes it a confirmation finding when a path of the composed script can return before its last commit step or, unless it declares `unverifiable`, without `verified()`. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact policy text needs recording again.
- `inspectAutofillStep` in `pomerado/core/destinations/autofill-step` takes an optional `trustSignInUrl(url: URL): boolean`, a host's own trust of more sign-in addresses, for a host whose user allowed a sign-in to go wherever the site sends it. Core asks it about each address of a sign-in step's fields, submit and popup that is neither on the site nor an `authenticationOrigins` entry, and only about one core would let a host trust: an https address with no user name or password, on a host with a registrable domain under a listed public suffix. Plain http, IP addresses, localhost, bare public suffixes, names only a private network resolves (under .localhost, .internal, .local, .test or .invalid) and hosts under .arpa or .onion stay refused, and the host is never asked about them. A card-fill step, and every part of a payment card, never use it. The inspection keeps the predicate as `trustSignInUrl`, and `fillAutofillStep` asks it again about a control that moved. Since it is also asked after typing, about addresses the page shaped, the host must never log, keep or forward a URL it is asked about. A predicate that throws trusts nothing. Refusal evidence names origins as before, so once anything was typed an origin no inspection judged is still named `other`. Unset, every check and every inspection is the same as before. `untrustedTarget`'s trust takes the same optional field.
- An inspection that passes with `trustSignInUrl` names, scheme, host and port alone, the origin of each address the predicate trusted, in `AutofillInspection.trustedSignInOrigins`, sorted and absent when it trusted none. It names a host first reached after typing too, such as the password screen an identifier-first sign-in hands off to, whose refusal evidence still names it `other`, so a host can keep the record of where its user's allowance sent the sign-in. A filled or uncertain `AutofillStepReport` names the same way any origin the predicate trusted when `fillAutofillStep` judged again a control the page moved. Each origin is within core's floor, and none is on the site or an `authenticationOrigins` entry. An origin the page shaped after typing may hold part of what was typed, so a host screens it as page text before it shows or forwards it. Where a credential may go is unchanged. Without `trustSignInUrl`, every inspection and report is the same as before.
- The cart skill's refusal of an order the site refuses as asked, such as one under its minimum, names the input: `new errors.InvalidInput(message, { field })`, `field` the input the caller would change.
- A write finishes every check that can refuse before its commit mark. The writes, cart, caller-input, core and publication skills and the workspace guide say that choosing a price-changing option, deciding an add-on or offer, reading the total and comparing it with a limit the input sets, and checking availability, the items, the address and the payment all happen before `enteringCommit`; the marked call only re-reads what a change would make wrong, clicks once and confirms. A refusal throws `InvalidInput` before the mark with `field`, a stable `kind` such as `over_price_limit` and the observed values in its message, so the run reports that it changed nothing; a write may instead return a refusal its output schema declares before its first mark (next entry). A refusal returned as output in a shape the schema does not declare, or returned or thrown after the mark, reports a possible change. A check that can only run after an earlier marked step runs before the later step's mark, and its refusal reports a possible change. The `write-session.ts` reference now checks its total against an optional `max_total_minor` input before its mark and guards the total inside the marked call.
  - A paid add-on or pre-selected paid option within the tool's purpose, the payment method and the delivery address are inputs or run-time questions. A checkout never uses the page's pre-selected address or payment silently, and never hard-codes a choice the build's request or the owner's answers settled, since the published tool serves other callers. A paid upsell outside the tool's purpose, such as a membership offered at checkout, is declined without a question and never accepted without an input the caller set.
  - The forms skill tells conditional pop-ups (shown only for some accounts, states or visits, such as a promotion or a membership upsell) from ones an action always opens: a conditional one is handled when it shows and never waited for or required. The `write-session.ts` reference declines a membership offer only some accounts get.
  - Guardian's publication policy adds a confirmation finding for a check that can refuse made inside or after the marked call of the commit it guards, or a refusal returned as output after a mark or in a shape the output schema does not declare (a declared refusal returned before the first mark, a re-read that stops before the click, and a check after an earlier marked step, are no finding), and a source-correction finding for code that requires a conditional pop-up. Its `input_option` finding covers the payment method, the delivery address and a hard-coded build-time choice, and names what is no finding: declining an out-of-scope upsell, a safety default, and dismissing a dialog with no paid or private choice. Guardian's execution review treats declining an out-of-scope paid upsell, or dismissing a dialog with no paid or private choice, as no missing input, so a live write step that does either is not escalated. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact policy text needs recording again.
- A write can declare refusals as part of its output contract. A branch of its output schema that requires `status` `"refused"` and a `reason` from a listed set of stable lowercase codes, with the values the caller needs, such as a total and the limit it exceeded, is a declared refusal. A run whose script returns one before it enters any declared commit step, and records no confirmation, completes with that output and reports the write not applied with no possible commit, where it used to fail as `outcome_unknown`. Returned after a commit step was entered, or in an undeclared shape, it still fails as `outcome_unknown`. A schema with another branch that could also match a `refused` status, such as a free-text `status`, declares no refusal. Throwing `InvalidInput` before the commit steps keeps working as before.
  - `pomerado/core/runtime/run-outcome` adds `declaredRefusalReasons`, `declaredRefusalOf` and `returnedRefusal`, which return the type `DeclaredRefusal`, with `refusedStatus`, `refusalReasonPattern` and `refusalReasonMaxLength`, and `RunEvidence` adds the optional `declared_refusal` flag. `confirmedRun` counts a `rejected` effect with valid output and `declared_refusal` as a settled result, which `writeStatusOf` reports `not_applied`, and `validatedWriteNeeds` returns `nothing` for a `rejected` effect. A host adopts it by passing a returned write's published output schema, output, commit evidence and runner effect to `returnedRefusal`, and recording `rejected` with `declared_refusal` when it finds one; the refusal's reason is in its output. A host that does not keeps classifying such runs as before.
  - Guardian's publication policy for a write build accepts a declared refusal returned before the first commit mark, which is no finding for a path that returns before its last commit step or without `verified()`, and makes one returned after a mark, for a condition the script never checks, or in an undeclared shape a confirmation finding. The writes, caller-input and publication skills describe the convention beside `InvalidInput` thrown before the mark. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact policy text needs recording again.
- Guardian's review of a blocked explanation reads a decision ledger the harness writes, so it judges the report against what the build's reviews decided and its live tests showed, not against what its own compacted conversation still holds. `reviewQuestion` receives the optional `decisionLedger` (type `DecisionLedger` in `pomerado/core/mint/contracts`) for a blocked explanation, and Guardian reads it as `question_review.decisionLedger`.
  - The ledger lists each Guardian denial or escalation of an execution, a live test batch or a publication in the build, with its outcome, reason, review id and the rationale and finding explanations Guardian wrote, screened as they are for the minter. It counts each live test case's latest verdict and lists the cases whose latest result did not pass. A failing case's error class and message sit under `toolReported`: they are what the agent's own tool code threw, and the policy says they show how the tool failed, never why the site behaved as it did. Only the denials, their review ids, the verdicts and the counts are host facts.
  - It keeps the latest 12 denials and 20 failing cases, caps each rationale at 800 characters, each of up to 4 finding explanations at 300, each message at 300 and each error class and result at 120, and counts what the bounds left out (`omittedDenials`, `omittedFindings`, `omittedTestFailures`).
  - `MintHarnessSnapshot` adds the optional `decisionLedger`, the denials so far, so a takeover keeps them. An attempt that restores a checkpoint without it, from an older worker, marks its ledger `partial`, and the policy says a denial missing from a partial ledger proves nothing. An older worker ignores the field.
  - `PendingQuestion` and `questionForReview` add the optional `decisionLedger`. A host that builds its `PendingQuestion` from named fields forwards `decisionLedger` to give its blocked reviews the ledger; one that does not keeps today's review. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact policy text needs recording again.
- Guardian's `read_source` tool reads up to 10 files in one call: with `path`, `offset` and `match` null, `paths` lists each file with its own `offset` and `match`. The result, `{ kind: "untrusted_sources", files }`, holds each file's chunk or matches in order; a file that cannot be read has its own `status: "unavailable"` entry and the others still return. Once a call's results reach 200,000 characters, its remaining files come back `not_read` for another call. Each file is its own read through the host's `readSource`, so it counts as Guardian's read for the entrypoint check, the publication round's whole-file reads and a session's unchanged sources, as a call naming only it would. A single-file call is unchanged, and a recorded call without `paths` still decodes.
  - The instructions every review kind shares ask Guardian to request the files it already knows it needs in as few calls as possible, up to 10 files each, and to read again only for files or chunks those calls show it needs; the core publication policy says the same.
  - A call the host cannot read, such as one naming more than 10 files or setting both `path` and `paths`, reads nothing and tells Guardian what to change, such as to split the files over several calls, instead of reporting the source unavailable. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact instructions or tool schema needs recording again.
  - `pomerado.hostProtocol` stays 1: no `./core/*` export changes, and a host's `readSource` serves each file as before.

- `lintSchemas(inputSchema, outputSchema)` in `pomerado/core/runtime/output-lint` adds two advisory checks of a tool's declared schemas, never of its values: `loose_text`, a required output string whose schema accepts an empty string or whitespace alone, and `looser_than_input`, an output field looser than the only input field of the same name (an enum dropped or widened, a format's pattern, one that refuses plain words, neither repeated nor replaced by an enum, a smaller `minLength`, or optional or nullable where the input is required). Neither blocks. A schema that refers to itself is walked once per definition, and a walk records at most 2,000 fields. `outputChecks` and `outputCheckMeaning` list both. `lintOutput` never reports them, so a run's output checks and telemetry are unchanged.
  - `outputNotes` from `pomerado/core/mint/output-notes` takes an optional `inputSchema` and adds these findings to `publication/output-notes.json`. The local host passes it at publication, and adds the findings to an example's output-check notice for the minter.
  - Guardian's `publicationOutputNotesPolicy` reads them as leads for the Loosened needed value rule, on needed values only. The rule itself now asks for every needed value's field to be judged in one pass, required, not nullable, non-empty and not whitespace alone, its format held by a pattern where it has one, and no looser than the input it reports, and a later review of the same build to judge what changed.
  - The core skill asks for inputs and outputs typed as strictly as their values, with `Schema.NonEmptyTrimmedString` for required output text and `Schema.NonEmptyString.pipe(Schema.pattern(/\S/u))` for required input text, which still takes a caller's stray space, a pattern for a date, time or code, and an output that reports an input's value reusing that input's enum or pattern.
- Guardian's publication review quotes a line or two of the text at fault without hunting for a unique quote: the host already places a quote at its first occurrence, or on the whole file, and the finding stands either way.
- Guardian's publication policy adds `publicationReferencePolicy`: the SDK's behavior is in `pomerado/browser/index.js` and `pomerado/runtime/*.js` and the minter's skills in `.agents/<name>/SKILL.md`, which the reviewer may read on demand where the host serves them; a path the host does not serve is not missing evidence. The local host serves the SDK's modules under `pomerado/`, a `.ts` or `.d.ts` spelling as the same-named `.js`, and its skills to the publication review through the new optional `references` of `PublicationReview` and `PublicationCandidate` in `pomerado/core/mint/publication-review`, which stay out of the publication index. `skillReferenceFiles` in `pomerado/core/mint/skills` lists a host's skills at those paths. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact policy text needs recording again.
- A host can choose Guardian's reasoning effort for each review. `specialize` in `GuardianModelOptions` from `pomerado/core/guardian/openai` may return `reasoningEffort`, `low` or `medium` (type `GuardianReasoningEffort` from `pomerado/core/guardian/model`), and the review's request uses it on the same model as every other review. A review whose host returns none runs at `medium`, as before. Core picks no effort itself. `GuardianUsage`, and so each review's `guardian.usage`, adds the optional `reasoningEffort` the review ran at. The request's effort can change the provider's hidden instructions, so a review at a different effort from the review before it may read the session's history without its cached prefix: compare `cachedTokens` across efforts before relying on it.
- `MintOutcome` adds the optional `stopReason` (type and schema `MintStopReason`, values in `mintStopReasons`), which names why an unpublished build stopped: `final_text_without_tool`, `turn_budget_spent`, `model_quota_exhausted`, `sign_in_failed`, `account_locked`, `question_unanswered`, `owner_declined`, `site_lacks_capability`, `policy` or `host_unavailable`. It also adds the optional `stopExplanation`: the minter's last answer when it stopped without a tool call, screened for credentials and host-private values and kept only when Guardian's question review of a blocked outcome allows it. `MintFailure` adds `turnBudgetSpent`, and `MintModel.run` may return `finalText` beside `stopReason`.
- A blocked build's `summary` carries the minter's explanation only when Guardian allowed its caller to read it.
- Guardian's publication review may return `publicReason`, one short plain sentence about why a tool was not published, apart from the private rationale. The shared output format adds the field, null for every other kind. `PublicationDenial` adds the optional `publicReason`, screened for credentials and host-private values and cut to `publicReasonLimit` (300) characters. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact output format or publication policy text needs recording again.
- The owner may decline a login. `CredentialQuestion` adds the optional `allowDecline`, and `CredentialAnswer` the optional `declined` (`LoginDecline`: `no_account` or `public_only`), which holds no login and saves nothing. `MintDependencies.requestLogin` may answer `no_account` or `public_only`, and a sign-in may fail with `MintFailure.loginDeclined`. The minter hears either as a `login_declined` result, never a failed sign-in, and builds the part of the request the site shows signed out; the host signs in no more and raises no further login request in the build. `MintHarnessSnapshot` adds the optional `loginDeclined`. The local host offers the decline on its first login request, and at the terminal an empty username declines.
- `MintDependencies.abandonSignIn` (optional, answering `AbandonSignInResult`: `abandoned`, or `refused` with a reason and notice) offers the minter `abandon_sign_in`, which gives up signing in so the build goes on signed out. It holds the execution permit, Guardian does not review it, and the host decides whether a build may: it refuses a write build, a task that needs the account and a verified sign-in. `MintActions` and `HostToolDescriptions` add the optional `abandonSignIn`.
- `report_blocked` no longer refuses with `sign_in_unresolved` once sign-in is unavailable while a retained receipt held the ending back: it ends the build with the sign-in's own result.
- Once a build abandoned its sign-in, through `abandon_sign_in` or an applied `login` change `signed_out`, it stays signed out: `authenticate` comes back `sign_in_abandoned` without running and a question routed to sign-in asks for no login, until a later applied `login` change `sign_in` or `different_login`. `MintHarnessSnapshot` adds the optional `signInAbandoned`, so a takeover keeps it.
- `MintDependencies.stopExplanationReviewMs` (optional, one minute by default) bounds the review of a minter's last answer; past it the outcome keeps no `stopExplanation`. A restored ending whose login check was unavailable reports `stopReason` `host_unavailable`. A login the owner declined records no sign-in execution, and after an earlier failed sign-in, a declined login request drops the failed sign-in's guidance.
- The auth skill tells the minter to report an account the site locked and what to do after a declined login, the core skill and workspace guide to ask early about a required value the site never shows, and the workspace guide and `report_blocked` description name the new reasons.
- Sign-in pieces the local host runs are exported for other hosts: the recorded rejection marker read (`autofillMarkerVisible`) from `pomerado/core/destinations/autofill-marker`, the signed-in marker's settle wait (`settledMarkerCheck`, `markerSettle`) from `pomerado/core/destinations/marker-settle`, the login prompt and notice texts (`credentialPrompt`, `credentialMessage`, `correctionTriesLeft`) from `pomerado/core/runtime/credential-prompts`, the rules for reusing an answer to a script's question (`scriptQuestionKeys`, `reusedScriptAnswers`, `keptScriptAnswers`, `forgetScriptAnswers` and the `KeptScriptAnswer` schema) from `pomerado/core/mint/script-answer-reuse`, and the login field helpers from `pomerado/core/runtime/login-values`. No prompt or notice text changes. The local host's `check_signed_in_marker` now stops checking a loaded page once its next half-second check would start more than 10 seconds after the first, rather than checking once more at the 10-second mark. Its rejection marker read no longer refuses a screen whose address carries a user name or password, and a recorded popup reported missing when the step recorded none fails the read rather than reading as no marker.
- A publication refused, fixed and submitted again in the same build is reviewed against what changed since its last review instead of from scratch. Guardian keeps each completed publication round, its decision, each file's digest and screened text within a bound, and which files a review read whole, in its session, and the next round's request adds `publication_delta`: the prior decision and findings with their quoted text, each indexed file as unchanged (and read whole), unchanged but never read whole (a search, which shows only slices around each match, doesn't count as a read: its reads still go through `ReviewTurn.readSource`, so a host that wraps it keeps seeing them, inside the new optional `ReviewTurn.uncountedReads`), changed (with a unified diff, or the whole file when that is shorter), added, removed or not compared, and the other request fields that changed. `publicationDeltaPolicy` follows the review's policy, a host's own included, and asks Guardian to check each prior finding against the change, review what changed, judge unread and uncompared files as in a first review, and leave only read, unchanged files alone unless a change makes them relevant. Every file stays readable and the outcomes are the same; a first round is unchanged.
  - `GuardianSessionSnapshot` from `pomerado/core/guardian/session` adds the optional `publicationRound`, so a host that saves and resumes the session keeps the record across a takeover with no change. It is stored loosely and decoded when used, so a round this release cannot read never fails the restore; it is dropped. An older snapshot has none, and its next round is reviewed as a first one.
  - `ReviewSources` from `pomerado/core/guardian/review` adds the optional `publicationDelta`, and `guardian.started` for a later round counts its files by state under `delta`. Every publication review reads every indexed file through the host's reader once, up to 4 MiB each and four at a time, without counting it as Guardian's read; a file longer than that is not compared. `wholeSource` from `pomerado/core/guardian/source` is joined by `completeSource`, which returns undefined rather than a prefix for such a file.
  - A host that sends small publication files inline in its own request can leave out the ones the delta lists as unchanged. A recorded Guardian response keyed on a later round's exact request text needs recording again.
- The minter asks one question round per form page. The forms skill now opens with that rule, so a builder reads it before its first question: before the first `act` step it asks up front for every field it can see or anticipate, and on each later page it first fills every value the request, the input and earlier answers supply, then asks everything still open on that page in one `request_input`. A field that appears only after an answer is still asked in place. The rule moved from the skill's "Multi-step forms" list, and the `forms.step-rules` section marker moved with it, still ahead of `forms.fixture-checks`. The writes skill's rule for an option the input does not settle points to it.
- On a write, the workspace guide prefers keeping the value the page already shows in an optional field that adds no cost over asking the caller to confirm or correct it. The value is listed in `assumptions` and stays an optional input. Add-ons, pre-selected paid options and saved payment are still asked. Guardian's question policy prefers reword, on a write too, for a question that only asks to confirm or correct such a value, or asks about a control with exactly one possible value. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact policy text needs recording again.
- The core skill's first bullets say to prefer a role locator's name from an ARIA snapshot over `textContent` or `innerText`, which include hidden labels, and to prefer `waitForOutcome`, with the step as its `action` and every result it can lead to named, over `waitForURL` or `waitForLoadState`, since a dialog keeps the URL and a route change without a new document passes `waitForLoadState` at once. The deeper paragraph that named every answer moved into that bullet, and the line on waiting after a navigation now prefers `waitForOutcome` with a control only the destination page has over waiting for the destination URL alone.
- The core skill's click bullets prefer waiting for the page a click opens, which may be a new tab, over waiting on the old page's URL, and prefer clicking a control's visible label when its input is hidden. A script that waited on the old page after a click opened a new tab timed out, and one that clicked a radio whose input a custom control hides failed.
- The minter may send several tool calls in one response, which saves model calls. Its build turns set `parallelToolCalls`, and the effect question turn does not. The host, not the model, decides how each call runs. Reads, `read_source` and `captcha_state`, sent together run at the same time, and each result goes back to its own call. Every other call, an `apply_patch` edit, `exec_command`, `execute`, `request_input`, `finish_build`, `report_blocked` or another host tool, runs alone in the model's order once the calls before it returned, and a read after one of them waits for it. `exec_command` stays ordered because the host cannot tell a command that only reads from one that writes, and every command already goes through review and the harness's one execution permit; an `execute` explore step may click or type, so browser calls stay ordered too. A function call after an edit in its response waits until the response's edits land and then runs with no model call between: the SDK runs function calls before edits, so the minter holds such a call as an approval, and its run loop approves it and resumes. Such a call runs only when every edit in its response came before it and applied, and otherwise comes back `not_run` with `effect: "not_sent"`, so a step never runs behind an edit that was refused. Every execution still gets its own review, and Guardian reviews the bytes the step runs. The `execute` tool description says to prefer sending a step's edit and its `execute` together, and to send a later call in the same response only when it does not depend on an earlier result. A host's model observer and recovery boundary may now see a response's reads at the same time, as may its source and capture readers and its CAPTCHA state reader; a host that serializes its recovery boundary runs them one at a time. A host's `reviewAndExecute` and workspace editor still see one call at a time.
- Once an `execute` or `exec_command` in a response does not succeed, no later call of that response runs. A step succeeds only with a `completed` receipt; one that failed, was refused by review, timed out or may have dispatched holds the calls after it, which come back `not_run` with `effect: "not_sent"` and say an earlier call in the response did not succeed, so the minter decides again with that result. An edit after it is held too and comes back failed with the same result. Reads started before the failed step are unaffected, and a call after `finish_build` or `report_blocked` in the same response still never runs. A call whose arguments did not parse never ran, so it holds nothing. The hold is derived from the response and its results, so a recovery that replays a retained response and its retained results holds the same calls; workspace tools now pass through the host's recovery boundary inside this ordering, as host tools already did, so a held call is never recorded there.
- The core skill points the minter at the site's other tools when `reference/site-tools.json` exists: it reads the index `reference/site-tools/README.md` first, then the per-tool folders it needs, each with a `README.md` and, unless the index omits it, the tool's full source. It reuses their identifiers, field names and shapes, and copies and adapts their code into its own `src/` files, but never imports, reads or references a file under `reference/` from its code, and checks what it copies against the site as it observes it now.
- The core skill says `waitForOutcome` returns the name of the answer that showed, and prefers branching on that name over waiting again for the same answers or rechecking them with `isVisible`. It also prefers the wait helpers' own settling over a tool's own loops that poll until values hold still, since `waitForRows` and `waitForValues` already return only once what they read is filled in and reads the same on two looks.
- The core skill says that when the site's own search has no match for the caller's value, the tool throws `InvalidInput` with `field` and `available`, the closest entries the site showed, rather than only naming them in the message, so the host can end the run at once with those choices.
- The core skill's input schema rules, beside the rule that a repair fixes a broken declared input, prefer fixing the whole class of a failure over its one instance: when the page requires a control the input has no value for, the tool prefers making every such control a declared caller question with the page's live options over throwing for it.
- The minter's `read_source` reads up to 10 files in one call. `paths` lists them, each with its own `offset` and `limit`, and `path`, `offset` and `limit` are null. Each file reads as a call naming only it would, with the same default and maximum length, and the results come back in order as `{ kind: "untrusted_sources", files }`. A file whose read fails returns its own failure in its place without failing the others. A call that is cancelled or times out stops before its next file, as a single read does. Once the results reach 200,000 UTF-16 code units, the files left come back as `not_read` with reason `call_size_limit`, unread. A call naming one `path`, including one recorded before `paths` existed, reads as before. The `read_source` tool description says so.
  - Each file is its own host read through `MintActions.readSource` and its own `ModelObserver.tool` dispatch, with that file's `readPath` and arguments, under the model's one tool call ID. A host that counts skill reads from `readPath` counts each file whose read returned, and no other. No host interface changes.
- The testing skill and the `live_tests` instructions tell a read signed out to rerun its failing cases together in one batch while a fix is in progress, not one batch each, since each batch waits on its own Guardian review. The testing skill and the `live_tests` tool description no longer say `maxWorkers` sets how many cases run at once: the `LiveTestHost` contract says a host may run a batch's cases on up to its own `maxWorkers` browsers at once, whatever `LiveTestBatch.workers` or the request's `maxWorkers` says. The `maxWorkers` field stays in the `live_tests` request, so older hosts and callers keep working.
- The testing skill prefers trying other records as one batch of live cases over one probe at a time. One Guardian review covers a batch, while each probe waits on its own review, so trying records one probe at a time slowed builds.
- The auth skill, beside the rule on recording an account choice, prefers exercising a chooser step from both starts in the build, right after a sign-in and from a saved session already past the chooser. A tool whose build ran its chooser step only right after sign-in failed on runs that started on a saved session, which skipped the chooser.
- The publication skill's checks before `finish_build` open with one line: before the first `finish_build`, prefer checking the tool against each rule the request states for its inputs, outputs and refusals. A build took several publication rounds whose refusals mostly restated rules its request had already stated, and each round costs minutes.
- The writes skill says a commit that went through, with the new record's id, already proves the write, and that a read-back after the commit compares the caller's text normalized as before it, through `normalizeText` from `pomerado/runtime`, since sites reformat what they save. It compares a chosen option's value only where the view shows that value, and its label otherwise, and prefers finding each value in the record by a stable id, its column header or a `data-` attribute over its cell's position.
- Guardian's "Unapplied supplied input" rule no longer flags a read's tailoring input whose description says the site picks the value when the caller gives none, or may apply another, which the output labels. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact policy text needs recording again.
- The core skill says an input that tailors the answer on a read is the exception to keeping required inputs required: it is optional unless the task can't run without it. The writes skill still keeps the inputs a write states required, while the core skill's guidance for tailoring inputs, optional unless the task can't run without them, still applies to any tool that offers one, a cart tool included. It also says `<input>_note` is an optional output field, set only when the site won't apply the caller's value.
- `LiveTestInconclusiveReason` in `pomerado/core/mint/live-tests` adds `site_block`: the site blocked the browser's network egress, such as an edge refusal of the page's own data call or a bot vendor's block page, so the case tested nothing about the tool. `judgeCase` judges it inconclusive and tells the minter it is an egress problem, not a script failure, so it does not change a working script for it. A host that maps every reason, such as to its own text, adds this one.
- A caller's answer to a design question about how an input is used is respected. An input that tailors the answer, such as a location or store, may have a limited use, such as a postal code used only to look up nearby locations without changing the selected one, when the caller chose that use. The minter asks it as a `request_input` choice question with id `input_use_<input>`, `<input>` being the input's name in lowercase snake_case, so a camelCase input such as `postalCode` is `input_use_postal_code`, offering the full use beside the limited ones, and records the chosen use in the input's description, starting "By design:".
  - Guardian's `publicationOutputPolicy` counts such a documented use as the input applied, not an unapplied supplied input, when `trusted_authority.answeredQuestions` holds the caller's answer under that id. A "By design:" description that misses or contradicts the answer, or that no answer backs, is still a finding, and so is an answer to another question. A filter, sort, date or other value that restricts the answer stays strict whatever an answer says. The "Unapplied input" rule points to the exception. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact policy text needs recording again.
  - `AnsweredQuestion` gains an optional `id`, the question's id in its request. `answersForReview` sets it, and Guardian's review input passes it in `trusted_authority.answeredQuestions`, so an answer binds to what it decides by id, not by prompt text. A host that builds its own answers may add it.
  - `PublicationScope` gains an optional `publishedInputs`, each `{ name, description? }`: the inputs the tool's published revision declares. A host that repairs a published tool may supply it from its own record of that revision, never from anything the minter wrote, and Guardian then lets a repair keep a "By design:" description that revision declared for the same input word for word, as the caller's earlier decision. Without it a repair needs the caller's answer in its own job, like a first build. The core review passes it in `trusted_publication`; the local host keeps no published revisions and supplies none.
  - The core skill replaces its rule that no answer makes a supplied value lookup-only with that exception, teaches a first build to ask the question when an input's use is genuinely ambiguous, and tells a repair to keep a "By design:" input's use and description word for word, changing the use only after asking the caller again. The workspace guide lists the question among the reasons to ask.
- The testing skill, the workspace guide and the `live_tests` instructions tell a read signed out to prefer rerunning only the failing cases while a fix is in progress, then to run every case once more after its last edit, before the example. A batch of every case waits on its slowest case, so rerunning the cases that already passed after each fix slowed builds without adding evidence. The publication record is unchanged: a case that ran before the last edit is still stale.
- The core skill says `readRows` and `waitForRows` read every returned row of a list in one call, and the search skill asks for the facts each card shows without a click.
- The testing skill tells the minter to stop reworking a click once a case fails at the same control after two fixes, usually a custom widget, and to apply that input through the results URL the site produced or the page's own data request in the HTTP version, reading it back from the site's committed state or the answer. The search skill allows either route when a control is unreliable. The search and testing skills also say that when setting one control reloads the result list, such as a location or store, it goes before the filters and sorts its reload would drop, and that after each reload the tool waits for the row identifiers or results header to change and settle, never for every card's value to hold.
- The http-mcp, testing and workspace guidance treat publishing a read's HTTP version alone as a normal outcome, not a fallback: once the HTTP version passes its tests and the example ran on it, the minter publishes `http` alone, even while the Playwright version is incomplete or fails a case, and stops fixing a Playwright version it will not ship. Inputs are still read back from the answer, and a version that does not ship is neither recorded nor judged. A repair of a read may finish the same way, including a tool that shipped only its Playwright version, while keeping the tool's inputs and outputs.
- `MintFailure` gains the publication reason `input_contract_changed` with `brokenInputs`, each `{ field, change }` (type `BrokenInput`) with `change` one of `removed`, `required` or `changed`. A host that compares a publication's input schema with the published tool's refuses one that changes a declared input by failing its `publish` with it. `finish_build` hands the minter exactly its fixed instruction with `brokenInputs`, neither a review nor an outage, and records the refusal with recovery `new_observation`: the minter restores each input, keeps every new input optional, runs the example again with the edited code and publishes that execution. Core ships no comparison, and the local host refuses none.
- Guardian's publication output policy adds "Unapplied supplied input": code or an output field that lets a run return results when a valid value the caller supplied did not apply, such as a filter, sort or date the page ignored or reset, is a `schema_mismatch` finding with reason `source_correction`. That covers an output flag such as `sort_applied: false`, results for the page's own default, or a "best-effort" or "lookup-only" description. Facts the page shows for the applied value, such as availability at the applied location, stay data. Neither the description nor an answer to a question excuses it. Returning the site's own default when the caller supplied no value, and saying so, stays fine. Inputs that tailor the answer, such as a location, store, delivery or pickup mode, vehicle, or membership or rate type, are the exception in a read: once the code has tried each way the captures show the site offering to set that value, returning the site's results with the value it actually applied, read back from the page and shown beside the caller's value with the site's message, is right, while skipping a way the captures show, hiding that the applied value differs or presenting the results as the caller's is the finding, fixed by trying those ways and labeling the result. So is code in a read that submits caller details in a step the captures show creating an account or a lasting record on the site. A write throws `InputNotApplied` for such an input before its first commit step. For any other input the fix stays `InputNotApplied` or `InvalidInput`. The live-tests policy counts a tailoring input applied, and one the site won't apply returned with the applied value, as the capabilities to test. 0.4.0 canaries named it "Unapplied supplied location" and covered locations only, refusing them. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact policy text needs recording again.
- The authoring skills treat inputs that tailor the answer, facts about the visitor such as where they are (a ZIP, address, store, or delivery or pickup), what they have (a vehicle, a device) or who they are (a membership or rate type, a plan, a business account), as inputs the tool offers when the site lets a visitor set one, optional unless the task cannot run without it.
  - Left out, the site's default applies and the tool returns the value the site used. Given, the tool tries each way the site offers, preferring one that needs nothing more from the caller, reads the applied value back and returns it as `applied_<input>`, such as `applied_store`.
  - When the site still won't apply the caller's value, or says it does not serve a location, a read returns what the site did show, with the applied value beside the requested one (`applied_<input>`, null only when the page shows none) and the site's message in `<input>_note`, never presented as the answer for the caller's value. A read no longer throws `InputNotApplied` for such an input; a write still does, before its first commit step. Filters, sort and dates stay strict and still throw `InputNotApplied`.
  - More detail the site needs to apply the input, such as a street address or a vehicle's trim, is its own optional input, described as sent to the site and submitted only when the caller gave it. A name, email or phone is offered only when the site won't apply the input without it. A step that creates an account or a lasting record on the site is a write, so a read stops before it. While building, a live step, the example's or a test case's, that enters a name, email or phone still asks the owner first, as the execution review requires, and the owner's answer keeps the build a read. The forms skill says a quote, contact or inquiry form that sends a name, email or phone creates a lead or other lasting record, so it is a write, while a step that only sets a tailoring input with details the caller gave, a name, email or phone the site requires for it included, stays part of the read unless it creates an account or a lasting record.
  - The search skill's location section lists the ways to try: the store picker, the ZIP box, the delivery or pickup choice, the manual entry beside "use current location", and declining the browser's location permission. A store missing from the picker, and a store and a ZIP that exclude each other, throw `InvalidInput`.
  - The core, search, caller-input, testing, publication, writes, forms and http-mcp skills and the workspace guide say so. A live test of a location the site won't apply expects a labeled result, not an error.
- The caller-input skill now owns validating a tool's input and choosing its error, and the minter reads it before settling any tool's inputs and errors. The input schema holds every rule it can express: types, enums, bounds, formats and required fields. Rules it cannot express, such as an end date on or after its start date, a count within the site's limits, inputs that exclude each other or at least one of a set, are checked before any site action and refused with `InvalidInput`. `InvalidInput` is for a value that is wrong or that the site does not offer, with `available`. `InputNotApplied` is for a valid, offered value the page did not apply. A greyed-out option on a read is unavailable data, not an error, and a write that may have changed the site is never retried.
- The authoring skills settle how a tool treats inputs the page does not take:
  - An option the page lists but greys out, such as a booked date or a sold-out size, is data on a read: the requested value unavailable, with the page's reason and alternatives, and the run succeeds. A write throws `InvalidInput` with `available`. A value the page does not list at all stays `InvalidInput` with `available`. Neither picks another value.
  - `available` for an input that depends on another, such as a check-out after a check-in, is read after the input it depends on applied. Rules the input alone breaks are checked before any site action.
  - A removed or not-found record page is a named outcome that ends in `InvalidInput` with the page's words, never a readiness timeout.
  - A number on a stepped scale, such as a price slider, snaps to the nearest step inside the caller's bound (down for a maximum, up for a minimum), and the tool returns the applied bound. The workspace guide's read-back rule names this and a disabled filter group as the two cases that return results with what applied. The "value the page does not offer" rule covers choice lists only, and a filter group the page disables for a result set is reported, never waited on.
  - Read-back text compares normalized for case and whitespace, locators never use generated CSS class names, and checks that values stopped changing ignore text that changes on its own, such as countdowns.
  - Search results keep related items, recommendations and duplicates out of the matches, and mark a card that stands for a group of variants.
- `report_blocked` takes the reason `host_unavailable` (`BlockedReport`) when the host has ended live execution and what the build still needs cannot run without it, such as the fresh example a source correction needs. It ends the attempt with the host's failure (`hostFailure: "host_unavailable"`), never as blocked, and runs no explanation review; while the host still offers live execution it is refused as `host_available`. `BuildBlocked` and the recorded `blocked` outcome are unchanged. The workspace guidance no longer says a retained receipt may take a source correction once live execution ended: only unchanged source publishes against it. The HTTP skill says to try an HTTP version while building, before the final example, so `finish_build`'s ask is only a backstop.
- One output check for every place that looks at a tool's output: `lintOutput` in `pomerado/core/runtime/output-lint`. It is deterministic and site-independent, and names each finding by field path (array positions folded to `[]`), check and count. It flags script code, style rules, markup and template leftovers, which block publication, and JSON held as text, invisible characters, untrimmed or very long strings, text that may have been read collapsed (a value ending in an expand control's words such as "Read more", the label of an expand control from `controlLabelsFromAriaSnapshot`, a button or on-page link such as "Show more" right after text, or an ending ellipsis on a page that offered one), one field restating its siblings (a whole card's text), repeated entries or records, and list fields that never vary or are always empty, which are flags for the publication review. The blocking checks need strong signals, such as a function or arrow body, a call or assignment on a DOM global, a statement-ending declaration, embedded JSON-LD, a selector block with style declarations, an HTML element's tag or an unreplaced placeholder, so specs, prices, policies and citations that run long with parentheses and semicolons are not flagged. Every pattern's work per character is bounded by a constant, and the leak checks read at most the first 16 KB of a value; a value that is JSON as a whole is never read as script. A field whose schema declares a code or markup `contentMediaType` (`text/javascript`, `text/css`, `text/html` and a few other named code types) keeps its findings, reported as not blocking with that type as their `override`, so the review sees them. The whole-card and repeated-words checks stop at a work budget on very large outputs and mark their findings `partial`.
  - The local host adds `outputChecks` to an execution's observations when its output has findings, with samples, so the minter fixes the read before publishing.
  - `finish_build` refuses a read example whose output still holds a blocking finding, with the new reason `output_checks_blocked` and `MintFailure.outputFindings`, before the contract review. `PublicationRequest.outputOverrides` lets the minter keep any flagged value that is correct as returned, such as code on a tool that returns code or page text that only resembles code, with a reason the publication review checks against the captures.
  - `outputNotes` and `outputNotesPath` in `pomerado/core/mint/output-notes` give hosts the same check at publication: the blocking findings, and `publication/output-notes.json`, every finding with each override and its reason, as host evidence for the publication review. Guardian's publication policy adds `publicationOutputNotesPolicy`, which treats every finding as a lead to verify, never a verdict. Its Lost information rule counts text that ends in or holds an expand control's label, or ends in an ellipsis, as trimmed only when the captures show a fuller copy on the page the tool read, never a snippet the site cuts short itself or an `include` section an example did not ask for.
- `pomerado/runtime` adds `visibleTextCode`, page code that defines `visibleText`, `visibleTexts` and `readRows`. They read only the text a person sees on the rendered page, never a hidden copy, a script, a style or a template, normalize whitespace and remove characters that never show (keeping the zero-width non-joiner and joiner, which spell words and join emoji), read a `display: contents` row through its rendered children, and fail with a `VisibleTextFailure` naming its reason and counts rather than fall back to hidden text. `as: "accessible"` reads what a screen reader reads instead. `readRows` reads a window of rows in one call, and fails as `hidden_only` when rows matched and none is rendered while one still has a box, such as rows fading in from `opacity: 0`; rows with no box at all, such as a no-results page's `hidden` or `display: none` template row, read as `[]`. `normalizeText` applies the same normalization to text outside the page.
- The authoring skills read values with `visibleText` or `readRows`, expand collapsed text before reading it, return each fact once, keep related records out of a list's results, mark a card that stands for a group as a group, and offer costly or bulky parts of a page through one optional `include` input, such as the site's filters, each record's whole text or related records.
- `waitCode` from `pomerado/runtime` declares the page waits `waitForOutcome`, `waitForRows`, `waitForValues` and `waitForChange` and `waitReport()`, over one set of budgets in `timeoutDefaults` (navigation 30 s, action 5 s, `answer` 8 s without progress, `answerCap` 30 s, `settle` 0.5 s, `unchanged` 2 s, `lateFillCap` 15 s). A wait keeps going while the page shows progress (a loading sign, DOM changes in a named `region`, the site's own document or API request in flight, a URL change, or its own reads filling) and fails with a readable reason once it shows none. `outcomeWaitCode` still declares only the names it declared before, `waitForOutcome` and its four `outcomeWait…` helpers, with the rest kept inside, so code that pastes it beside helpers of its own still parses; paste one of the two, never both. A generic loading sign (a busy region, an indeterminate progress bar, a skeleton, shimmer, spinner or loader class) counts only while it is new, and determinate bars and form controls never do, so a page's decoration holds a wait for at most the no-progress window; the site's own sign passed as `loading` counts for as long as it shows. `waitForRows` also waits while a visible sign shows beside its rows, never inside one: one there before the action, in the same number, is the page's own, and any other counts until it and the rows have held for `noProgressMs` with no request the action started in flight, so a sign that never leaves costs one window, never the cap. A request the wait's `action` started counts until it ends, up to `lateFillCap`, and the page then gets `unchangedMs` to render; any other request keeps the page busy for at most 4 s, since long-polls and streams stay open. A request repeated with the same method, URL and body at a steady interval is a poll and is ignored, and requests to one API path with different bodies are not. Without `siteDomain`, the site is the page host's last two labels, or three under a country's shared second level such as `co.uk`. `waitForRows` reads each field from its first visible match and reports a needed field, or a row's key, whose matches are all hidden as `hidden`. `waitForOutcome` adds `noProgressMs`, `loading`, `region` and `siteDomain` and the reason `outcome_unknown`, and its `timeout` defaults to the 30 s cap while the page keeps progressing; its `outcome_timeout` message now also names the progress it saw.
- Guardian's execution policy and the publication finding "Built URL" allow a tool to open a URL built from the site's own pattern, as the authoring skill now describes: a URL the site's controls produced, with only the parts that carry each input filled from the caller's values, through `URLSearchParams` for a query part or `encodeURIComponent` for a path part, every input read back from the page, and the page's controls run once instead when the landing is not an expected answer or a read-back differs. A guessed parameter or route, iterating on URL variants, an opaque filter code the page did not produce in that run, and a built URL without a read-back stay denied. A recorded Guardian response keyed on the exact policy text needs recording again.
- A tool's call that ends with `outcome_unknown`, `values_loading`, `values_timeout` or `change_unknown` now fails as a `BrowserActionTimeout`, as `outcome_timeout` does.
- `probeCallCode` from `pomerado/core/runtime/host-execute` wraps an exploration probe's call so its actions default to the 5 s action budget, and the local host applies it to every call of an `explore` step.
- A read that has not signed in tests itself: the minter designs live test cases from what it saw on the site, runs them in batches and reads every result. Only a read signed out tests beyond its example; a signed-in read and a write prove the tool with one run (see the breaking change on test scope).
